| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Donald_Trump_series)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Donald_Trump_series)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Donald_Trump_series) |

[Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s first tenure as the [president of the United States](24113.md) began on January 20, 2017, when he [was inaugurated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump) as the [45th president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States), and ended on January 20, 2021. Trump, a [Republican](32070.md), took office after defeating [Democratic](5043544.md) nominee [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election). Upon his inauguration, he became the first president in American history [without prior public office or military background](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States_by_previous_experience). Trump made an unprecedented number of [false or misleading statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_or_misleading_statements_by_Donald_Trump) during his [2016 campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign) and first presidency. Alongside Trump's first presidency, the Republican Party also held their majorities in the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) and the [Senate](24909346.md) during the [115th U.S. Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/115th_United_States_Congress) following the [2016 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_elections), thereby attaining an overall federal [government trifecta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_trifecta#United_States). Trump's first presidency ended following his re-election defeat to Democratic nominee [Joe Biden](145422.md) in [2020](48410011.md).

Trump signed the [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act), the [First Step Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Step_Act), and a [partial repeal of the Dodd–Frank Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Growth,_Regulatory_Relief,_and_Consumer_Protection_Act). He appointed [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch), [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh), and [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett) to the [Supreme Court](31737.md). Trump sought substantial spending cuts to major welfare programs, including [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_\(United_States\)) and [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid). He was unsuccessful in his efforts to repeal the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act), but rescinded the [individual mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_shared_responsibility_provision). Trump reversed numerous [environmental regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration), and [withdrew from the Paris Agreement on climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_and_the_Paris_Agreement#First_withdrawal). He enacted various [tariffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariffs_in_the_first_Trump_administration), triggering retaliatory tariffs from [China](57055272.md), Canada, Mexico, and the European Union. He withdrew from the [Trans-Pacific Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Pacific_Partnership) negotiations and signed the [United States–Mexico–Canada Agreement](58620732.md) (USMCA), a successor to the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) with modest changes. Trump oversaw the third-biggest [federal deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_deficit) growth of any president; it significantly increased under Trump due to simultaneous spending increases and tax cuts.

Starting in 2018, Trump implemented a [controversial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controversy) [family separation policy for migrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_administration_family_separation_policy) apprehended at the United States–Mexico border. His demand for the federal funding of [a border wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall) resulted in [the longest U.S. government shutdown in history](59474007.md), a record that stood until [2025](81225965.md), under his [second term](78298108.md). In 2020, he [deployed federal law enforcement forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_deployment_of_federal_forces_in_the_United_States) in response to [racial unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_racial_unrest_\(2020%E2%80%932023\)).

Trump's "[America First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_First_\(policy\))" [foreign policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration) was characterized by unilateral actions and a disregard for traditional norms and foreign allies. His administration implemented [a major arms sale to Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_States%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_arms_deal); [denied citizens from six Muslim-majority countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13769) entry into the United States; [recognized Jerusalem as the capital of Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_recognition_of_Jerusalem_as_the_capital_of_Israel); and brokered the [Abraham Accords](64883396.md), a series of [normalization agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_normalization) between Israel and various [Arab states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world). Trump withdrew United States troops from northern Syria, allowing [Turkey to occupy the area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Turkish_offensive_into_north-eastern_Syria). His administration [made a conditional deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal) with the [Taliban](30635.md) to [withdraw United States troops from Afghanistan in 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_United_States_troops_from_Afghanistan_\(2020%E2%80%932021\)). Trump met [North Korea](21255.md)'s leader [Kim Jong Un](5962511.md) three times. He [withdrew the United States from the Iran nuclear agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_withdrawal_from_the_Iran_nuclear_deal) and later escalated tensions in the Persian Gulf by [ordering the assassination](62726668.md) of Iranian general [Qasem Soleimani](16805217.md).

[Robert Mueller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Mueller)'s [Special Counsel investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_special_counsel_investigation) (2017–2019) [concluded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_report) that [Russia interfered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2016_United_States_elections) to favor Trump's candidacy in the 2016 election and that, while the prevailing evidence "did not establish that members of the Trump campaign conspired or coordinated with the Russian government", possible obstructions of justice occurred during the course of that investigation. Trump [attempted to pressure Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Trump%E2%80%93Ukraine_scandal) to announce investigations into Biden. This triggered Trump's [first impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump) by the House of Representatives on December 18, 2019; he was [acquitted by the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_trial_of_Donald_Trump) on February 5, 2020.

Trump reacted slowly to the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md), ignored or contradicted many recommendations from health officials in [his messaging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communication_of_the_Trump_administration_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic), and [promoted misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation_by_the_United_States) about unproven treatments and the availability of testing. After signing the [CARES Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CARES_Act), Trump initiated [Operation Warp Speed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Warp_Speed) to facilitate and accelerate the development, manufacturing, and distribution of [COVID-19 vaccines](63319438.md).

Following his loss in 2020 to Biden, Trump alleged widespread [electoral fraud](382165.md) and initiated an [extensive campaign to overturn the results](65905202.md). At a rally on January 6, 2021, Trump urged his supporters to march to the [Capitol](31979.md), where the [electoral votes were being counted by Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count) in order to formalize Biden's victory. A mob of Trump supporters [stormed the Capitol](66307490.md), suspending the count and causing [Vice President](32759.md) [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) and other members of Congress to be evacuated. On January 13, the House voted to [impeach Trump an unprecedented second time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump) for [incitement of insurrection](324091.md), but he was later [acquitted by the Senate again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_trial_of_Donald_Trump) on February 13, after he had already left office.

Trump [later ran for re-election again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_former_presidents_of_the_United_States_who_ran_for_office) in [2024](59568546.md), and he was successfully re-elected to a second, non-consecutive term, after defeating Democratic nominee [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris). He started his [second presidency](78298108.md) on January 20, 2025, as the 47th president, thus becoming the second former U.S. president to return to office.[^cite_note-1]

## Milestones

### 2016 election

Main articles: [Donald Trump 2016 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign), [2016 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), and [2017 United States Electoral College vote count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count)

Further information: [2016 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_elections), [2016 Republican Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries), and [2016 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_National_Convention)



2016 Electoral College vote results. Five individuals besides Trump and Clinton received electoral votes from [faithless electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faithless_electors_in_the_2016_United_States_presidential_election).

Trump announced [his candidacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2016_presidential_campaign) for the [nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries) of the [Republican Party](32070.md) in the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) on June 16, 2015. In May 2016, Trump secured the Republican nomination. [Trump selected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_Party_vice_presidential_candidate_selection) Governor [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) of Indiana as his [running mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Running_mate), and the two were officially nominated at the [2016 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Republican_National_Convention).

Early on November 9, 2016, the day after the election, Trump was projected to have secured the presidency. Trump won the [presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) with 304 electoral votes and 46.1% of the popular vote, while [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) received 227 electoral votes and 48.2% of the popular vote.[^cite_note-2] Trump thus became the fifth person to [win the presidency while losing the popular vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_elections_in_which_the_winner_lost_the_popular_vote).[^cite_note-3] The [electoral votes were certified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count) on January 6, 2017. In the concurrent [congressional elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_elections), Republicans secured a [government trifecta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_trifecta) after retaining their majorities in the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) and the [Senate](24909346.md), and [Speaker of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives) [Paul Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Ryan) and [Senate Majority Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_leaders_of_the_United_States_Senate) [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell) both remained in their posts.

### Transition period and inauguration

Main articles: [First presidential transition of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_presidential_transition_of_Donald_Trump) and [First inauguration of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump)



Incumbent president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) and President-elect Trump in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office) on November 10, 2016



[Chief Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Justice) [John Roberts](1928850.md) administers the [presidential oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office_of_the_president_of_the_United_States) to Trump at the [Capitol](31979.md), January 20, 2017

The [presidential transition period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_presidential_transition_of_Donald_Trump) began following Trump's victory in the [2016 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), though Trump had chosen [Bill Hagerty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Hagerty) to begin planning for the transition in August 2016. During the transition period, Trump announced nominations for his [cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_cabinet_of_Donald_Trump) and [administration](44029.md).

Trump was [inaugurated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump) on January 20, 2017, officially assuming the presidency at 12\:00 pm, EST. He was sworn in by [Chief Justice](31739.md) [John Roberts](1928850.md).[^cite_note-inaugurated1-4] In his seventeen-minute inaugural address, Trump painted a dark picture of contemporary America, pledging to end "American carnage" caused by urban crime and saying America's "wealth, strength, and confidence has dissipated" by jobs lost overseas.[^cite_note-5] He declared his strategy would be "[America First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_First_\(policy\))."[^cite_note-inaugurated1-4] The largest single-day protest in U.S. history, the [Women's March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Women's_March), took place the day after his inauguration and was driven by opposition to Trump and his policies and views.[^cite_note-6]

### First 100 days

Main article: [First 100 days of the first Trump presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_100_days_of_the_first_Trump_presidency)

The [first hundred days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_hundred_days_\(United_States\)) of a President's term have been historically significant since the first term of [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) who, in his first 100 days, worked on both legislative and executive actions to combat [the great depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_great_depression).[^cite_note-7] In Trump's first hundred days, he signed 24 [executive orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_order_\(United_States\)),[^cite_note-8] 22 [presidential memoranda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_memoranda), 20 [presidential proclamations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_proclamation), and 28 [bills](52047975.md).[^cite_note-9]

Near the end of the 100 days, the Trump administration introduced a broad outline of a sweeping [tax reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_reform) focusing on deep [tax cuts](455990.md). Although Trump had to concede to delay funding for [the U.S.–Mexico border wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico%E2%80%93United_States_border_wall#First_Trump_administration_\(2017%E2%80%932021\)) he had promised, narrowly avoiding a [government shutdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_shutdown_in_the_United_States) a few days before the end of the first 100 days.[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11]

Further information: [Political appointments of the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_appointments_of_the_first_Trump_administration)

The Trump administration was characterized by record turnover, particularly among White House staff. By early 2018, 43% of senior White House positions had turned over.[^cite_note-12] The administration had a higher turnover rate in the first two and a half years than the five previous presidents did over their entire terms.[^cite_note-13]

By October 2019, one in 14 of Trump's political appointees were former lobbyists; less than three years into his presidency, Trump had appointed more than four times as many lobbyists than his predecessor [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) did over the course of his first six years in office.[^cite_note-14]

Trump's cabinet included U.S. senator from Alabama [Jeff Sessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Sessions) as [attorney general](44002.md),[^cite_note-15] banker [Steve Mnuchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Mnuchin) as [Treasury Secretary](43338.md),[^cite_note-16] retired Marine Corps general [James Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Mattis) as [Defense Secretary](43998.md),[^cite_note-17] and [ExxonMobil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ExxonMobil) CEO [Rex Tillerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rex_Tillerson) as [Secretary of State](32293.md).[^cite_note-18] Trump also brought on board politicians who had opposed him during the presidential campaign, such as neurosurgeon [Ben Carson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Carson) as [Secretary of Housing and Urban Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development),[^cite_note-19] and South Carolina governor [Nikki Haley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikki_Haley) as [Ambassador to the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Nations).[^cite_note-20]

### Cabinet

Main articles: [First cabinet of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_cabinet_of_Donald_Trump) and [List of dismissals and resignations in the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_dismissals_and_resignations_in_the_first_Trump_administration)

See also: [Vice presidency of Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vice_presidency_of_Mike_Pence)

Days after the presidential election, Trump selected RNC Chairman [Reince Priebus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reince_Priebus) as his [chief of staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Chief_of_Staff).[^cite_note-21] Trump chose Sessions for the position of attorney general.[^cite_note-22]

In February 2017, Trump formally announced his cabinet structure, elevating the [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md) and [Director of the Central Intelligence Agency](1777424.md) to cabinet level. The [Chair of the Council of Economic Advisers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Economic_Advisers#Chairmen_and_members), which had been added to the cabinet by Obama in 2009, was removed from the cabinet. Trump's cabinet consisted of 24 members, more than Obama at 23 or [George W. Bush](3414021.md) at 21.[^cite_note-23]

On February 13, 2017, Trump fired [Michael Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Flynn) from the post of [National Security Advisor](44028.md) on grounds that he had lied to Vice President Pence about his communications with [Russian Ambassador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_Russia_to_the_United_States) [Sergey Kislyak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Kislyak); Flynn later pleaded guilty to lying to the [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md) (FBI) about his contacts with Russia.[^cite_note-24] Flynn was fired amidst the ongoing controversy concerning [Russian interference in the 2016 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2016_United_States_elections) and accusations that Trump's electoral team colluded with Russian agents.

In July 2017, [John F. Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kelly), who had served as [secretary of Homeland Security](212926.md), replaced Priebus as chief of staff.[^cite_note-25] In September 2017, [Tom Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Price_\(American_politician\)) resigned as [Secretary of HHS](44279.md) amid criticism over [his use of private charter jets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Price_\(American_politician\)#Private_jet_scandal_and_resignation) for personal travel.[^cite_note-26] [Kirstjen Nielsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kirstjen_Nielsen) succeeded Kelly as secretary in December 2017.[^cite_note-27] Secretary of State [Rex Tillerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rex_Tillerson) was fired via a tweet in March 2018; Trump appointed [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo) to replace Tillerson and [Gina Haspel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gina_Haspel) to succeed Pompeo as the director of the CIA.[^cite_note-28] In the wake of a series of scandals, [Scott Pruitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Pruitt) resigned as [Administrator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrator_of_the_Environmental_Protection_Agency) of the [Environmental Protection Agency](58666.md) (EPA) in July 2018.[^cite_note-29] Secretary of Defense [Jim Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Mattis) informed Trump of his resignation following Trump's abrupt December 19, 2018, announcement that the remaining 2,000 American troops in Syria would be withdrawn, against the recommendations of his military and civilian advisors.[^cite_note-30]

Trump fired numerous [inspectors general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inspector_general) of agencies, including those who were probing the Trump administration and close Trump associates. In 2020, he [fired](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_dismissals_of_inspectors_general) five inspectors general in two months. *The Washington Post* wrote, "For the first time since the system was created in the aftermath of the Watergate scandal, inspectors general find themselves under systematic attack from the president, putting independent oversight of federal spending and operations at risk."[^cite_note-31]

### Dismissal of James Comey

Main article: [Dismissal of James Comey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dismissal_of_James_Comey)

Trump dismissed [FBI Director](5861065.md) [James Comey](2156690.md) on May 9, 2017, saying he had accepted the recommendations of Attorney General Sessions and [Deputy Attorney General](2933681.md) [Rod Rosenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Rosenstein) to dismiss Comey. Sessions's recommendation was based on Rosenstein's, while Rosenstein wrote that Comey should be dismissed for his handling of the conclusion of the FBI investigation into the [Hillary Clinton email controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton_email_controversy).[^cite_note-32] On May 10, Trump met Russian foreign minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) and Russian ambassador [Sergey Kislyak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Kislyak) in the Oval Office. Based on White House notes of [the meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump's_disclosures_of_classified_information), Trump told the Russians, "I just fired the head of the FBI. He was crazy, a real nut job ... I faced great pressure because of Russia. That's taken off."[^cite_note-33] On May 11, Trump said in a videoed interview, "... regardless of recommendation, I was going to fire Comey ... in fact, when I decided to just do it, I said to myself, I said, you know, this Russia thing with Trump and Russia is a made-up story."[^cite_note-34] On May 18, Rosenstein told members of the U.S. Senate that he recommended Comey's dismissal while knowing Trump had already decided to fire Comey.[^cite_note-35] In the aftermath of Comey's firing, the events were compared with those of the "[Saturday Night Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturday_Night_Massacre)" during [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon)'s administration and there was debate over whether Trump had provoked a [constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_crisis), as he had dismissed the man leading an investigation into Trump's associates.[^cite_note-36] Trump's statements raised concerns of potential obstruction of justice.[^cite_note-37] In [Comey's memo about a February 2017 meeting with Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dismissal_of_James_Comey#February_14_meeting), Comey said Trump attempted to persuade him to abort the investigation into Flynn.[^cite_note-38]

## Judicial appointments

Further information: [List of federal judges appointed by Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Donald_Trump) and [Donald Trump judicial appointment controversies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_judicial_appointment_controversies)



Supreme Court nominee [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett) and her family with Trump on September 26, 2020

After Republicans won control of the U.S. Senate in 2014, only 28.6 percent of judicial nominees were confirmed, "the lowest percentage of confirmations from 1977 to 2018".[^cite_note-39] At the end of the Obama presidency, 105 judgeships were vacant.[^cite_note-40] Senate Republicans, led by Senate Majority Leader [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell), prioritized confirming Trump's judicial appointees, doing so rapidly.[^cite_note-41] By November 2018, Trump had appointed 29 judges to the [U.S. courts of appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._courts_of_appeals), more than any modern president in the first two years of a presidential term.[^cite_note-42]

Trump ultimately appointed 226 [Article III federal judges](834368.md) and [260 federal judges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Donald_Trump) in total.[^cite_note-43] His appointees, who were usually affiliated with the conservative [Federalist Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalist_Society), [shifted the judiciary to the right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_appointment_history_for_United_States_federal_courts).[^cite_note-Ruiz-44] A third of Trump's appointees were under 45 years old when appointed, far higher than under previous presidents.[^cite_note-Ruiz-44] Trump's judicial nominees were less likely to be female or ethnic minority than those of the previous administration.[^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46] Of Trump's judicial appointments to the U.S. courts of appeals (circuit courts), two-thirds were white men, compared to 31% of Obama nominees and 63% of George W. Bush nominees.[^cite_note-Ruiz-44][^cite_note-47]

### Supreme Court nominations

Main article: [Donald Trump Supreme Court candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_Supreme_Court_candidates)

Trump made three nominations to the [Supreme Court](31737.md): [Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch), [Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh), and [Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett):

- [Trump nominated Neil Gorsuch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Gorsuch_Supreme_Court_nomination) in January 2017 to fill the vacancy left by the death of Justice [Antonin Scalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonin_Scalia) in February 2016, which had not been filled by Obama because the Republican-majority Senate did not consider the [nomination of Merrick Garland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrick_Garland_Supreme_Court_nomination). The Senate confirmed Gorsuch in a mostly [party-line vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party-line_vote) of 54–45 in April 2017.

  [^cite_note-48]

  &#x20;Gorsuch's confirmation was one of Trump's major first year accomplishments, made as part of a "100‑day pledge".

  [^cite_note-Jacobson-49]

- [Trump nominated Brett Kavanaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Kavanaugh_Supreme_Court_nomination) in July 2018 to replace retiring Justice [Anthony Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Kennedy), who was considered a key swing vote on the Supreme Court. The Senate confirmed Kavanaugh in a mostly party-line vote of 50–48 in October 2018 after allegations that Kavanaugh had attempted to rape another student when they were both in high school, which Kavanaugh denied.

  [^cite_note-50]

  [^cite_note-51]

- [Trump nominated Amy Coney Barrett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amy_Coney_Barrett_Supreme_Court_nomination) in September 2020 to fill the vacancy left by the [death of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg) [Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg). Ginsburg was considered part of the Court's liberal wing and her replacement with a conservative jurist substantially changed the [ideological composition of the Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideological_leanings_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_justices).

  [^cite_note-52]

  &#x20;Democrats opposed the nomination, arguing that the court vacancy should not be filled until after the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md). On October 26, 2020, the Senate confirmed Barrett by a mostly party-line vote of 52–48, with all Democrats opposing her confirmation.

  [^cite_note-53]

## Leadership style

See also: [Rhetoric of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetoric_of_Donald_Trump)

Political scientist [Daniel W. Drezner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_W._Drezner) writes in *The Toddler in Chief* that Trump's staff and allies frequently characterized Trump as infantile.[^cite_note-54] He was reported to not read briefing documents, such as the [President's Daily Brief](584611.md), preferring oral briefings.[^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] Briefs would be kept short and mention his name repeatedly to keep his attention, and use visuals: "pictures, maps, charts and graphs".[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-time.com-58] He would also watch up to eight hours of television each day, especially [Fox News](11121.md) programs, and use their talking points in tweets.[^cite_note-59][^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] He was reportedly angry if the information contradicted his views and statements.[^cite_note-time.com-58]

He reportedly fostered chaos as a management technique, resulting in low morale and confusion among staff.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63] He was unable to compromise during the [115th U.S. Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/115th_U.S._Congress), which led to [gridlock](3786577.md) and few legislative accomplishments, despite Republican control of both houses of Congress.[^cite_note-64] Presidential historian [Doris Kearns Goodwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doris_Kearns_Goodwin) found Trump lacked several traits of an effective leader, including humility, empathy, and collaboration, among others[^cite_note-65]

*[Axios](52436164.md)* reported in 2018 and 2019 that his working hours were less than the beginning of his presidency, with fewer meetings and more unstructured free time, labelled "executive time".[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67]

### False and misleading statements

Main article: [False or misleading statements by Donald Trump (first term)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_or_misleading_statements_by_Donald_Trump_\(first_term\))



[Fact-checkers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fact-checkers) from *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*

[^cite_note-WashPostDatabase-68]

&#x20;(orange), the *[Toronto Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toronto_Star)*

[^cite_note-TorontoStar_20190605-69]

&#x20;and [CNN](62028.md)

[^cite_note-Dale_20200309-70]

[^cite_note-Dale_Subramaniam_3/9/2020-71]

&#x20;(blue) compiled data on "false or misleading claims", and "false claims", respectively. The peaks corresponded in late 2018 to [the midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_United_States_elections), in late 2019 to his [impeachment inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_against_Donald_Trump), and in late 2020 to the presidential election. The *Post* reported 30,573 false or misleading claims in four years,

[^cite_note-WashPostDatabase-68]

&#x20;an average of more than 20.9 per day.

The number and scale of Trump's statements identified as false by scholars, fact-checkers, and commentators was characterized as unprecedented for an American president,[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73] and even in U.S. politics.[^cite_note-74] *[The New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker)* called falsehoods a distinctive part of his political identity,[^cite_note-75] while Republican political advisor [Amanda Carpenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amanda_Carpenter) described them as a [gaslighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaslighting) tactic.[^cite_note-76] His White House had dismissed the idea of [objective truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Objective_truth),[^cite_note-77] and his campaign and presidency have been described as being "[post-truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-truth)",[^cite_note-78] and hyper-[Orwellian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orwellian).[^cite_note-79] He disregarded data from federal institutions that was incompatible to his arguments; quoted hearsay, anecdotal evidence, and questionable claims in partisan media; denying reality (including his own statements); and distracting when falsehoods were exposed.[^cite_note-80]

*[The Washington Post](102226.md)*'s [fact-checking](595273.md) team wrote in 2017 that they could not keep up with "the pace and volume of the president's misstatements."[^cite_note-81] They found that he made more than 30,000 false or misleading claims during his first presidency.[^cite_note-82] Common examples included the economy and jobs, his border wall proposal, his tax legislation, as well as statements regarding prior administrations.[^cite_note-83] Other topics included crime, terrorism, immigration, Russia and the investigation, the [Ukraine probe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump%E2%80%93Ukraine_scandal), and the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md).[^cite_note-WashPostDatabase-68] Senior administration officials also regularly gave false or misleading statements,[^cite_note-Dawsey-2017-84][^cite_note-85] which made it difficult for the news media to take official statements seriously.[^cite_note-Dawsey-2017-84]

### Rule of law

Some scholars concluded that during Trump's first presidency, largely due to his actions and rhetoric, the U.S. has experienced [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding).[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] Prominent Republicans have expressed similar concerns that Trump's perceived disregard for the rule of law betrayed conservative principles.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89][^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91]

Early on, Trump wanted the [Department of Justice](52563.md) to investigate Hillary Clinton,[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] the [Democratic National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Committee),[^cite_note-94] and James Comey.[^cite_note-95] He repeated allegations, some of which had already been investigated or debunked.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] White House counsel [Don McGahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_McGahn) advised Trump in 2018 such action would constitute abuse of power and invite possible [impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_impeachment_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-98] Trump demanded in May 2018 an investigation into whether or not his campaign was infiltrated or surveilled for "political purposes", which was referred to the DOJ [inspector general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice_Office_of_the_Inspector_General). While it is not illegal, prior presidents have avoided attempts to influence the Department of Justice to prevent perceptions of political interference.[^cite_note-nytimes.com2-99][^cite_note-100]

Attorney General Sessions resisted demands by Trump and his allies for such investigations, which frustrated Trump.[^cite_note-101] While criticizing the special counsel investigation in July 2019, Trump falsely claimed that [the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_Two_of_the_United_States_Constitution) ensures that "I have to the right to do whatever I want as president."[^cite_note-102] Trump had on multiple occasions either suggested or promoted views of extending his presidency beyond normal term limits.[^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104]

*Bloomberg News* reported in October 2019 that Trump had asked Secretary of State Rex Tillerson to pressure the Justice Department to drop a criminal investigation of [Reza Zarrab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza_Zarrab), which Tillerson refused.[^cite_note-105]

He frequently criticized the judiciary for "unfairly" interfering in his administration's ability to decide policy.[^cite_note-106] Chief Justice Roberts criticized in November 2018 Trump's characterization of a judge who had ruled against him as an "Obama judge".[^cite_note-107] Twenty Republican former [U.S. attorneys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Attorney), including some Republicans, characterized Trump in October 2020 as "a threat to the rule of law in our country." One stated that Trump "views the Justice Department and the FBI as his own personal law firm and investigative agency."[^cite_note-108]

### Relationship with the news media

Further information: [Donald Trump's conflict with the media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump's_conflict_with_the_media)



Trump talks to the press in the Oval Office on March 21, 2017, before signing [S.422](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_of_NASA) (the NASA Transition Authorization Act).



Trump speaks to reporters on the White House South Lawn in June 2019.

Early into his presidency, Trump developed a highly contentious relationship with the news media, repeatedly referring to them as the "[fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news) media" and "the [enemy of the people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enemy_of_the_people)."[^cite_note-109] As a candidate, Trump had refused press credentials for offending publications but said he would not do so if elected.[^cite_note-110] Trump both privately and publicly mused about taking away critical reporters' [White House press credentials](570541.md).[^cite_note-Stelter-2018-111] At the same time, the Trump White House gave temporary press passes to far-right [pro-Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-Trump) fringe outlets, such as *[InfoWars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/InfoWars)* and *[The Gateway Pundit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gateway_Pundit)*, which are known for publishing hoaxes and [conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory).[^cite_note-Stelter-2018-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113]

On his first day in office, Trump falsely accused journalists of understating the size of the crowd at his inauguration and called the news media "among the most dishonest human beings on earth." Trump's claims were notably defended by Press Secretary [Sean Spicer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sean_Spicer), who claimed the inauguration crowd had been the biggest in history, a claim disproven by photographs.[^cite_note-114] Trump's senior adviser [Kellyanne Conway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kellyanne_Conway) then defended Spicer when asked about the falsehood, saying it was an "[alternative fact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_fact)", not a falsehood.[^cite_note-115]

The administration frequently sought to punish and block access for reporters who broke stories about the administration.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] Trump frequently criticized right-wing media outlet Fox News for being insufficiently supportive of him,[^cite_note-120] threatening to lend his support for alternatives to Fox News on the right.[^cite_note-121] On August 16, 2018, the Senate unanimously passed a resolution affirming that "the press is not the enemy of the people."[^cite_note-122]

The relationship between Trump, the news media, and fake news has been studied. One study found that between October 7 and November 14, 2016, while one in four Americans visited a [fake news website](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_website), "Trump supporters visited the most fake news websites, which were overwhelmingly pro-Trump" and "almost 6 in 10 visits to fake news websites came from the 10% of people with the most conservative online information diets."[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] [Brendan Nyhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brendan_Nyhan), one of the authors of the study, said in an interview, "People got vastly more misinformation from Donald Trump than they did from fake news websites."[^cite_note-125]



During a joint news conference, Trump said he was "very proud" to hear [Brazilian](3383.md) president [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) use the term "fake news."

[^cite_note-126]

In October 2018, Trump praised U.S. representative [Greg Gianforte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greg_Gianforte) for assaulting political reporter [Ben Jacobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Jacobs_\(journalist\)) in 2017.[^cite_note-127] According to analysts, the incident marked the first time the president has "openly and directly praised a violent act against a journalist on American soil."[^cite_note-128] Later that month, as [CNN and prominent Democrats were targeted with mail bombs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_2018_United_States_mail_bombing_attempts), Trump initially condemned the bomb attempts but shortly thereafter blamed the "Mainstream Media that I refer to as Fake News" for causing "a very big part of the anger we see today in our society."[^cite_note-129]

The Trump Justice Department obtained by court order the 2017 [phone logs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phone_log) or email [metadata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metadata) of reporters from CNN, *The New York Times*, *The Washington Post*, [BuzzFeed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BuzzFeed), and *Politico* as part of investigations into leaks of classified information.[^cite_note-130]

### Twitter

Main articles: [Twitter use by Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter_use_by_Donald_Trump) and [Social media use by Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media_use_by_Donald_Trump)

Trump continued his use of [Twitter](9988187.md) following the presidential campaign. He continued to personally tweet from [@realDonaldTrump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/@realDonaldTrump), his personal account, while his staff tweet on his behalf using the official [@POTUS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/@POTUS) account. His use of Twitter was unconventional for a president, with his tweets initiating controversy and becoming news in their own right.[^cite_note-131] Some scholars have referred to his time in office as the "first true Twitter presidency."[^cite_note-132] The Trump administration described Trump's tweets as "official statements by the President of the United States."[^cite_note-133] The federal judge [Naomi Reice Buchwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naomi_Reice_Buchwald) ruled in 2018 that Trump's blocking of other Twitter users due to opposing political views violated the First Amendment and he must unblock them.[^cite_note-134] The ruling was upheld on appeal.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136]



Twitter activity of Donald Trump from his first tweet in May 2009 to September 2017. Retweets are not included.

His tweets have been reported as ill-considered, impulsive, vengeful, and [bullying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bullying), often being made late at night or in the early hours of the morning.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] His tweets about a Muslim ban were successfully turned against his administration to halt two versions of travel restrictions from some Muslim-majority countries.[^cite_note-140] He has used Twitter to threaten and intimidate his political opponents and potential political allies needed to pass bills.[^cite_note-141] Many tweets appear to be based on stories Trump has seen in the media, including far-right news websites such as [Breitbart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breitbart_News) and television shows such as *Fox & Friends*.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143]

Trump used Twitter to attack [federal judges](834368.md) who ruled against him in court cases[^cite_note-144] and to criticize officials within his own administration, including then-[Secretary of State](32293.md) [Rex Tillerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rex_Tillerson), then-[National Security Advisor](44028.md) [H. R. McMaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._R._McMaster), [Deputy Attorney General](2933681.md) [Rod Rosenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Rosenstein), and, at various times, Attorney General Jeff Sessions.[^cite_note-Lee-2016-145] Tillerson was eventually fired via a tweet by Trump.[^cite_note-146] Trump also tweeted that his [Justice Department](52563.md) is part of the [American "deep state"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_state_in_the_United_States);[^cite_note-147] that "there was tremendous leaking, lying and corruption at the highest levels of the FBI, Justice & [State](31975.md)" [Departments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_federal_executive_departments);[^cite_note-Lee-2016-145] and that the [special counsel investigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_special_counsel_investigation) is a "[WITCH HUNT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch_hunt)!"[^cite_note-148] In August 2018, Trump used Twitter to write that Attorney General Jeff Sessions "should stop" the special counsel investigation immediately; he also referred to it as "rigged" and its investigators as biased.[^cite_note-149]

| **((Twitter Safety))** (@TwitterSafety) tweeted: |
| --- |

> After close review of recent Tweets from the @realDonaldTrump account and the context around them we have permanently suspended the account due to the risk of further incitement of violence.

January 8, 2021[^cite_note-TweetTwitterSafety-150]&#x20;

&#x20;In February 2020, Trump tweeted criticism of the prosecutors' proposed sentence for Trump's former aide [Roger Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Stone). A few hours later, the Justice Department replaced the prosecutors' proposed sentence with a lighter proposal. This gave the appearance of presidential interference in a criminal case and caused a strong negative reaction. All four of the original prosecutors withdrew from the case; more than a thousand former Department of Justice lawyers signed a letter condemning the action.[^cite_note-151][^cite_note-152] On July 10, Trump commuted the sentence of Stone days before he was due to report to prison.[^cite_note-153]

In response to the mid-2020 [George Floyd protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Floyd_protests), some of which resulted in looting,[^cite_note-154] Trump tweeted on May 25 that "when the looting starts, the shooting starts." Not long after, Twitter restricted the tweet for violating the company's policy on promoting violence.[^cite_note-155] On May 28, Trump signed an executive order which sought to limit legal protections of social media companies.[^cite_note-156]

On January 8, 2021, Twitter announced that they had permanently suspended Trump's personal account "due to the risk of further [incitement](1816440.md) of violence" following the [Capitol attack](66307490.md).[^cite_note-157] Trump announced in his final tweet before the suspension that he would not attend the [inauguration of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden).[^cite_note-158] Other social media platforms like [Facebook](7529378.md), [Snapchat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snapchat), [YouTube](3524766.md) and others also suspended the official handles of Donald Trump.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160]

## Domestic policy

See also: [Social policy of the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration)

This section is an excerpt from [Domestic policy of the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration).\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Domestic_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration\&action=edit)]

Trump had mixed success in delivering on his domestic policy campaign promises, which included limiting immigration, fortifying public infrastructure, cutting taxes, and repealing the [Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Care_Act).[^cite_note-161] He also worked to encourage space exploration, implement the [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act), work on [deregulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation), address economic growth and unemployment, and work on trade.[^cite_note-162]

Trump was also in office during [COVID-19](63136490.md), and [directed responses to the pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communication_of_the_Trump_administration_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic).[^cite_note-163] President Trump also handled relief for three severe hurricanes and several large wildfires and signed the [Disaster Recovery Reform Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disaster_Recovery_Reform_Act).[^cite_note-164]

## Foreign policy

Main article: [Foreign policy of the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration)



[Trump made 19 international trips](52890158.md) to 24 different countries during his first presidency.

[^cite_note-165]



Trump and [North Korea](21255.md)'s [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workers'_Party_of_Korea) leader [Kim Jong Un](5962511.md) shake hands at the [Korean Demilitarized Zone](328874.md), June 30, 2019

The [foreign policy positions expressed by Trump during his presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_Donald_Trump_during_the_2016_presidential_election) changed frequently, so it was "difficult to glean a political agenda, or even a set of clear, core policy values ahead of his presidency".[^cite_note-166] Under a banner of "America First", the Trump administration distinguished itself from past administrations with frequent open admiration of authoritarian rulers and rhetorical rejections of key human rights norms.[^cite_note-167]

Despite pledges to reduce the number of active duty U.S. military personnel deployed overseas, the number was essentially the same three years into Trump's presidency as they were at the end of Obama's.[^cite_note-168]

In August 2019, Trump cancelled a state visit to [Denmark](76972.md) by invitation of [Queen Margrethe II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margrethe_II_of_Denmark) due to [Danish Prime Minister](273720.md) [Mette Frederiksen](26040044.md) having called Trump's [suggestion to purchase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposals_for_the_United_States_to_purchase_Greenland#First_presidency) [Greenland](12118.md), a territory within the [Danish Realm](11746442.md), "an absurd discussion".[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174]

On October 27, 2019, ISIS leader [Abu Bakr al-Baghdadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi) killed himself and three children by detonating a [suicide vest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_vest) during the [Barisha raid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barisha_raid) conducted by the U.S. [Delta Force](328809.md) in Syria's northwestern [Idlib Province](7445043.md).[^cite_note-175]

Trump withdrew from the [Open Skies Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Open_Skies), a nearly three-decade old agreement promoting transparency of military forces and activities.[^cite_note-176]

### Defense

Further information: [Foreign policy of the first Trump administration § Military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_policy_of_the_first_Trump_administration#Military)



Trump and Vice President [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) at the welcoming ceremony for Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff [Mark Milley](38091005.md) (left) on September 30, 2019. Outgoing chairman General [Joseph Dunford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Dunford) (right) and Secretary of Defense [Mark Esper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Esper) (center-right) are present

As a candidate and as president, Trump called for a major build-up of American military capabilities. Trump announced in October 2018 that the United States would withdraw from the [Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intermediate-Range_Nuclear_Forces_Treaty) with Russia. The goal was to enable the United States to counter increasing Chinese intermediate nuclear missile capabilities in the Pacific.[^cite_note-177] In December 2018, Trump complained about the amount the United States spends on an "uncontrollable [arms race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arms_race)" with Russia and China. Trump said that the \$716 billion which the United States was spending on the "arms race" was "Crazy!". He had previously praised his own increased defense spending, five months earlier. The total fiscal 2019 defense budget authorization was \$716 billion, although missile defense and nuclear programs made up about \$10 billion of the total.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]

During 2018, Trump falsely asserted that he had secured the largest defense budget authorization ever, the first military pay raise in ten years, and that [military spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_budget_of_the_United_States) was at least 4.0% of GDP, "which got a lot bigger since I became your president".[^cite_note-180]

Controversy arose in November 2019 after Trump pardoned or promoted three soldiers accused or convicted of [war crimes](48626.md).[^cite_note-181] The most prominent case involved [Eddie Gallagher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Gallagher_\(Navy_SEAL\)), a Navy SEAL team chief who had been reported to Navy authorities by his own team members for [sniping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sniper) at an unarmed civilian girl and an elderly man. Gallagher faced [court martial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_martial) for the murder of a wounded teenage combatant, among other charges. The medic of his SEAL team was granted immunity to testify against him, but on the witness stand the medic reversed what he had previously told investigators and testified that he himself had murdered the teenage combatant. Gallagher was subsequently acquitted of the murder charge against him, and the Navy demoted him to the lowest possible rank due to his conviction on another charge. The Navy later moved to strip Gallagher of his [Trident pin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Warfare_insignia) and to eject him from the Navy. Trump intervened to restore Gallagher's rank and pin. Many military officers were enraged by Trump's intervention, as they felt it disrupted principles of military discipline and justice. [Secretary of the Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_the_Navy) [Richard Spencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_V._Spencer) protested Trump's intervention and was forced to resign; in his resignation letter, he sharply rebuked Trump for his judgment in the matter. Trump told a rally audience days later, "I stuck up for three great warriors against the [deep state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_state_in_the_United_States)."[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184]

The Trump administration sharply increased the frequency of [drone strikes](38212231.md) compared to the preceding Obama administration, in countries such as Afghanistan, Iraq, Somalia, Syria and Yemen,[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] rollbacked transparency in reporting drone strike deaths,[^cite_note-crawford-187] and reduced accountability.[^cite_note-188] In March 2019, Trump ended the Obama policy of reporting the number of civilian deaths caused by U.S. drone strikes, claiming that this policy was unnecessary.[^cite_note-189]

### Afghanistan

Main article: [War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)](19666611.md)



From left: U.S. National Security Advisor [Robert O'Brien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_C._O'Brien_\(attorney\)), Vice President [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence), President [Donald Trump](4848272.md), Defense Secretary [Mark Esper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Esper), Chairman [Mark Milley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_A._Milley), and Brigadier General [Marcus S. Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcus_S._Evans) overseeing [Operation Kayla Mueller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Abu_Bakr_al-Baghdadi) in the [White House Situation Room](925990.md), October 26, 2019

The number of U.S. troops deployed to Afghanistan decreased significantly during Trump's presidency. By the end of Trump's term in office troop levels in Afghanistan were at the lowest levels since the early days of the war in 2001.[^cite_note-190] Trump's presidency saw an expansion of drone warfare and a massive increase in civilian casualties from airstrikes in Afghanistan relative to the Obama administration.[^cite_note-crawford-187]

In February 2020, [the Trump administration signed a deal with the Taliban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Taliban_deal), which if upheld by the Taliban, would result in the [withdrawal of United States troops from Afghanistan](66883576.md) by May 2021 (Trump's successor Joe Biden later extended the deadline to September 2021).[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192] As part of the deal, the U.S. agreed to the release of 5,000 Taliban members who were imprisoned by the Afghan government; some of these ex-prisoners went on to join the [2021 Taliban offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Taliban_offensive) that felled the Afghan government.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194]

In 2020, U.S. casualties in Afghanistan reached their lowest level for the entire war.[^cite_note-195] In Iraq, casualties increased, being significantly higher in Trump's term than Obama's second term.[^cite_note-196]

Following the collapse of the Afghan government and the [fall of Kabul](68481047.md) in August 2021, accusations by [Olivia Troye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivia_Troye) surfaced on [Twitter](9988187.md) of the Trump Administration deliberately obstructing the visa process for Afghans who had helped U.S. efforts in Afghanistan.[^cite_note-197]

### China

Main article: [United States foreign policy toward the People's Republic of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_foreign_policy_toward_the_People's_Republic_of_China)

On January 19, 2021, Secretary of State [Mike Pompeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pompeo) announced that the Department of State had determined that "genocide and crimes against humanity" had been [perpetrated by China against](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Uyghurs_in_China) the [Uyghur](31783.md) [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China) and other [ethnic minorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_minorities_in_China) in [Xinjiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinjiang).[^cite_note-wsj._U.S._says-198] The announcement was made on the last day of Trump's presidency. The incoming president, [Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden_administration), had already declared during his [presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2020_presidential_campaign), that such a determination should be made.[^cite_note-wsj._U.S._says-198] On January 20, 2021, Pompeo along with other Trump administration officials were sanctioned by China.[^cite_note-199]

### North Korea

Main article: [North Korea–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korea%E2%80%93United_States_relations)

See also: [2017–2018 North Korea crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932018_North_Korea_crisis) and [2018–19 Korean peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018%E2%80%9319_Korean_peace_process)

After initially adopting a verbally hostile posture[^cite_note-200] toward [North Korea](21255.md) and its leader, [Kim Jong Un](5962511.md), Trump quickly pivoted to embrace the regime, saying he and Kim "fell in love".[^cite_note-201] Trump engaged Kim by meeting him at two summits, [in June 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_North_Korea%E2%80%93United_States_Singapore_Summit) and [February 2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_North_Korea%E2%80%93United_States_Hanoi_Summit), an unprecedented move by an American president, as previous policy had been that a president's simply meeting with the North Korean leader would legitimize the regime on the world stage. During the June 2018 summit, the leaders signed a vague agreement to pursue denuclearization of the Korean peninsula, with Trump immediately declaring "There is no longer a Nuclear Threat from North Korea."[^cite_note-202] Little progress was made toward that goal during the months before the February 2019 summit, which ended abruptly without an agreement, hours after the White House announced a signing ceremony was imminent.[^cite_note-203] During the months between the summits, a growing body of evidence indicated North Korea was continuing its nuclear fuel, bomb and missile development, including by redeveloping an [ICBM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICBM) site it was previously appearing to dismantle – even while the second summit was underway.[^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] In the aftermath of the February 2019 failed summit, the Treasury department imposed additional sanctions on North Korea. The following day, Trump tweeted, "It was announced today by the U.S. Treasury that additional large scale Sanctions would be added to those already existing Sanctions on North Korea. I have today ordered the withdrawal of those additional Sanctions!"[^cite_note-208] On December 31, 2019, the [Korean Central News Agency](632399.md) announced that Kim had abandoned his moratoriums on nuclear and intercontinental ballistic missile tests, quoting Kim as saying, "the world will witness a new strategic weapon to be possessed by the DPRK in the near future."[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210] Two years after the Singapore summit, the North Korean nuclear arsenal had significantly expanded.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]

During a [June 2019 visit to South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Koreas%E2%80%93United_States_DMZ_Summit), Trump visited the [Korean Demilitarized Zone](328874.md) and invited North Korean leader Kim Jong Un to meet him there, which he did, and Trump became the first sitting president to step inside North Korea.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-215]

### Turkey

Main article: [Turkey–United States relations](5380399.md)



Trump with Turkish president [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan), May 16, 2017

In October 2019, after Trump spoke to Turkish president [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan), the White House acknowledged that Turkey would be carrying out a [planned military offensive into northern Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Turkish_offensive_into_north-eastern_Syria); as such, [U.S. troops in northern Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American-led_intervention_in_the_Syrian_civil_war) were withdrawn from the area to avoid interference with that operation. The statement also passed responsibility for the area's captured [ISIS](9087364.md) fighters to Turkey.[^cite_note-216] Congress members of both parties denounced the move, including Republican allies of Trump like Senator [Lindsey Graham](363279.md). They argued that the move betrayed the American-allied Kurds, and would benefit ISIS, Russia, Iran and Bashar al-Assad's Syrian regime.[^cite_note-217] Trump defended the move, citing the high cost of supporting the Kurds, and the lack of support from the Kurds in past U.S. wars.[^cite_note-218] Within a week of the U.S. pullout, Turkey proceeded to attack Kurdish-controlled areas in northeast Syria.[^cite_note-219] Kurdish forces then announced an alliance with the Syrian government and its Russian allies, in a united effort to repel Turkey.[^cite_note-220]

### Iran

Main article: [Iran–United States relations during the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93United_States_relations_during_the_first_Trump_administration)

During the first Trump administration, U.S.–Iran relations deteriorated sharply as Trump abandoned [Obama's engagement strategy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93United_States_relations_during_the_Obama_administration). The [U.S. withdrew from the nuclear deal (JCPOA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_withdrawal_from_the_Iran_nuclear_deal),[^cite_note-221] reimposed sanctions, and launched a [maximum pressure campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_pressure_campaign) that imposed over 1,500 sanctions and severely damaged Iran's economy.[^cite_note-222] In response, Iran escalated uranium enrichment[^cite_note-limit-223] and ruled out talks.[^cite_note-nowar-224] Tensions rose through 2019 with oil tanker attacks, [a downed U.S. drone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Iranian_shoot-down_of_American_drone), and cyber retaliation.[^cite_note-cyber-225] The crisis peaked in early 2020 after the U.S. [killed](62726668.md) Iranian General [Qasem Soleimani](16805217.md),[^cite_note-slain-226] prompting Iranian missile strikes. Relations remained hostile through the end of Trump's term.

### Saudi Arabia

Main article: [Saudi Arabia–United States relations](9753135.md)

See also: [2017 United States–Saudi Arabia arms deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_States%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_arms_deal)



Trump with Prince [Mohammad bin Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_bin_Salman), Washington, D.C., March 14, 2017

Trump actively supported the [Saudi Arabian-led intervention in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen) against the [Houthis](17240332.md).[^cite_note-prince-227][^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229] Trump also praised his relationship with [Saudi Arabia](349303.md)'s powerful Crown Prince [Mohammad bin Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad_bin_Salman).[^cite_note-prince-227] On May 20, 2017, Trump and [Saudi Arabia](349303.md)'s [King Salman bin Abdulaziz Al Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Salman_bin_Abdulaziz_Al_Saud) signed a series of letters of intent for the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia to purchase arms from the United States totaling \$110 billion immediately,[^cite_note-230] and \$350 billion over ten years.[^cite_note-cnbc-20170520-231][^cite_note-232] The transfer was widely seen as a counterbalance against the influence of Iran in the region[^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] and a "significant" and "historic" expansion of [United States relations with Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_relations_with_Saudi_Arabia).[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237][^cite_note-cnbc-20170520-231][^cite_note-238] By July 2019, two of Trump's three vetoes were to overturn bipartisan congressional action related to Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-239]

In October 2018, amid widespread condemnation of Saudi Arabia for the murder of prominent Saudi journalist and dissident [Jamal Khashoggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_Khashoggi), the Trump administration pushed back on the condemnation.[^cite_note-240] After the CIA assessed that Saudi crown prince Mohammad bin Salman ordered the [murder of Khashoggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Jamal_Khashoggi), Trump rejected the assessment and said the CIA only had "feelings" on the matter.[^cite_note-241]

### Israel / Palestine

Main articles: [Israel–United States relations](3449549.md), [Palestine–United States relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine%E2%80%93United_States_relations), [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md), [Gaza–Israel conflict](16346266.md), and [Israeli–Palestinian peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_peace_process)



Trump with Israeli prime minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](56472.md), February 15, 2017

Since the [Six Day War](29328.md) in 1967, the United States had considered [Israeli settlements](15123.md) in the occupied [West Bank](33209.md) to be "illegitimate". This status changed in November 2019 when the Trump administration shifted U.S. policy and[^cite_note-242] declared "the establishment of Israeli civilian settlements in the West Bank is not *per se* inconsistent with international law."[^cite_note-243]

Trump unveiled his own [peace plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Israel%E2%80%93Palestine_peace_plan) to resolve the [Israeli–Palestinian conflict](46216.md) on January 28, 2020.[^cite_note-244] A step toward improved relations in the region occurred in August 2020 with the first of the [Abraham Accords](64883396.md), when Israel and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) agreed to begin normalizing relations in [an agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93United_Arab_Emirates_normalization_agreement) brokered by [Jared Kushner](6191053.md), an accomplishment described by *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)* as "arguably his administration's first unqualified diplomatic success".[^cite_note-245][^cite_note-246] The following month, Israel and [Bahrain](18933277.md) agreed to [normalize diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahrain%E2%80%93Israel_normalization_agreement) in another deal mediated and brokered by the Trump administration.[^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] A month later, Israel and [Sudan](27421.md) [agreed to normalize relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Sudan_normalization_agreement) in a third such agreement in as many months. On December 10, 2020, Trump announced that Israel and [Morocco](19291.md) had agreed to [establish full diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Morocco_normalization_agreement), while also announcing that the United States recognized Morocco's claim over the [disputed territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_status_of_Western_Sahara) of [Western Sahara](33220.md).[^cite_note-250]

### United Arab Emirates

Main article: [United Arab Emirates–United States relations](15997134.md)

As Donald Trump lost the election bid against Joe Biden, the U.S. State Department notified Congress about its plans to sell 18 sophisticated armed [MQ-9B aerial drones](4769988.md) to the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md), under a deal worth \$2.9 billion. The drones were expected to be equipped with maritime radar, and the delivery was being estimated by 2024.[^cite_note-251] Besides, another informal notification was sent to the Congress regarding the plans of providing the UAE with \$10 billion of defense equipment, including precision-guided munitions, non-precision bombs and missiles.[^cite_note-252]

## Russia and related investigations

See also: [Links between Trump associates and Russian officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Links_between_Trump_associates_and_Russian_officials), [Business projects of Donald Trump in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_projects_of_Donald_Trump_in_Russia), and [Operation Crossfire Hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Crossfire_Hurricane)



Robert Mueller in the Oval Office c. 2012

American intelligence sources found the [Russian government](9787373.md) [attempted to intervene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2016_United_States_elections) in the 2016 presidential election to favor the election of Trump,[^cite_note-253] and that members of Trump's campaign were in contact with Russian government officials both before and after the election.[^cite_note-254] In May 2017, the Department of Justice appointed [Robert Mueller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Mueller) as special counsel to [investigate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_special_counsel_investigation) "any links and/or coordination between Russian government and individuals associated with the campaign of President Donald Trump, and any matters that arose or may arise directly from the investigation".[^cite_note-255]

During his January 2017 confirmation hearings as the attorney general nominee before the Senate, then-Senator Jeff Sessions appeared to deliberately omit two meetings he had in 2016 with Russian ambassador [Sergey Kislyak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Kislyak), when asked if he had meetings involving the 2016 election with Russian government officials. Sessions later amended his testimony saying he "never met with any Russian officials to discuss issues of the campaign".[^cite_note-256] Following his amended statement, Sessions recused himself from any investigation regarding connections between Trump and Russia.[^cite_note-257]

In May 2017, Trump discussed [highly classified intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_revelation_of_classified_information_to_Russia) in an [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office) meeting with the Russian foreign minister [Sergey Lavrov](541346.md) and ambassador [Sergey Kislyak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Kislyak), providing details that could expose the source of the information and how it was collected.[^cite_note-NYRosenberg-258] A Middle Eastern ally[^cite_note-260] provided the intelligence which had the highest level of classification and was not intended to be shared widely.[^cite_note-NYRosenberg-258] *The New York Times* reported, "sharing the information without the express permission of the ally who provided it was a major breach of espionage etiquette, and could jeopardize a crucial [intelligence-sharing](46556073.md) relationship."[^cite_note-NYRosenberg-258] The White House, through [National Security Advisor](44028.md) [H. R. McMaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._R._McMaster), issued a limited denial, saying the story "as reported" was incorrect[^cite_note-261] and that no "intelligence sources or methods" were discussed.[^cite_note-262] McMaster did not deny that information had been disclosed.[^cite_note-263] The following day Trump said on Twitter that Russia is an important ally against terrorism and that he had an "absolute right" to share classified information with Russia.[^cite_note-264] Soon after the meeting, American intelligence extracted a high-level covert source from within the Russian government, on concerns the individual could be at risk due, in part, to Trump and his administration repeatedly mishandling classified intelligence.[^cite_note-265]

In October 2017, former Trump campaign advisor [George Papadopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Papadopoulos) pleaded guilty to one count of making false statements to the FBI regarding his contacts with Russian agents. During the campaign he had tried repeatedly but unsuccessfully to set up meetings in Russia between Trump campaign representatives and Russian officials.[^cite_note-266]

Trump went to great lengths to keep details of his private conversations with Russian president [Putin](32817.md) secret, including in one case by retaining his interpreter's notes and instructing the linguist to not share the contents of the discussions with anyone in the administration. As a result, there were no detailed records, even in classified files, of Trump's conversations with Putin on five occasions.[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268]

Of Trump's campaign advisors and staff, six of them were indicted by the special counsel's office; five of them ([Michael Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Cohen_\(lawyer\)), [Michael Flynn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Flynn), [Rick Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Gates_\(political_consultant\)), [Paul Manafort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Manafort), [George Papadopoulos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Papadopoulos)) pleaded guilty, while one has pleaded not guilty ([Roger Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Stone)).[^cite_note-269] As of December 2020, Stone, Papadopoulos, Manafort, and Flynn have been pardoned by Trump, but not Cohen or Gates.[^cite_note-270]

On June 12, 2019, Trump asserted he saw nothing wrong in accepting intelligence on his political adversaries from foreign powers, such as Russia, and he could see no reason to contact the [FBI](11127.md) about it. Responding to a reporter who told him FBI director [Christopher Wray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_A._Wray) had said such activities should be reported to the FBI, Trump said, "the FBI director is wrong." Trump elaborated, "there's nothing wrong with listening. If somebody called from a country, Norway, 'we have information on your opponent' – oh, I think I'd want to hear it." Both Democrats and Republicans repudiated the remarks.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274]

*The New York Times* reported in June 2021 that in 2017 and 2018 the Justice Department subpoenaed [metadata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metadata) from the [iCloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ICloud) accounts of at least a dozen individuals associated with the [House Intelligence Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Intelligence_Committee), including that of ranking Democratic member [Adam Schiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Schiff) and [Eric Swalwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Swalwell), and family members, to investigate leaks to the press about contacts between Trump associates and Russia. Records of the inquiry did not implicate anyone associated with the committee, but upon becoming attorney general [Bill Barr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Barr) revived the effort, including by appointing a federal prosecutor and about six others in February 2020. *The Times* reported that, apart from corruption investigations, subpoenaing communications information of members of Congress is nearly unheard-of, and that some in the Justice Department saw Barr's approach as politically motivated.[^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276] Justice Department Inspector General [Michael Horowitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_E._Horowitz) announced an inquiry into the matter the day after the *Times* report.[^cite_note-277]

### Special counsel's report

Main article: [Mueller report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_report)
