In February 2018, when Mueller indicted more than a dozen Russians and three entities for interference in the 2016 election, Trump asserted the indictment was proof his campaign did not collude with the Russians. *The New York Times* noted Trump "voiced no concern that a foreign power had been trying for nearly four years to upend American democracy, much less resolve to stop it from continuing to do so this year".[^cite_note-278]

In July 2018, the special counsel indicted twelve Russian intelligence operatives and accused them of conspiring to interfere in the 2016 U.S. elections, by hacking servers and emails of the Democratic Party and the [Hillary Clinton 2016 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton_2016_presidential_campaign).[^cite_note-279] The indictments were made before [Trump's meeting with Putin in Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Russia%E2%80%93United_States_Summit), in which Trump supported Putin's denial that Russia was involved and criticized American law enforcement and intelligence community (subsequently Trump partially walked back some of his comments). A few days later, it was reported that Trump had actually been briefed on the veracity and extent of Russian cyber-attacks two weeks before his inauguration, back in December 2016, including the fact that these were ordered by Putin himself. The evidence presented to him at the time included text and email conversations between Russian military officers as well as information from a source close to Putin.[^cite_note-280]



The [redacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitization_\(classified_information\)) version of the Mueller report was released to the public by the [Department of Justice](52563.md) on April 18, 2019

On March 22, 2019, Mueller submitted the final report to Attorney General William Barr. Two days later, Barr sent Congress a [four-page letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barr_letter), describing what he said were the special counsel's principal conclusions in the report. Barr added that, since the special counsel "did not draw a conclusion" on obstruction,[^cite_note-281] this "leaves it to the Attorney General to determine whether the conduct described in the report constitutes a crime".[^cite_note-282] Barr continued: "Deputy Attorney General [Rod Rosenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rod_Rosenstein) and I have concluded that the evidence developed during the Special Counsel's investigation is not sufficient to establish that the President committed an obstruction-of-justice offense."[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284]

On April 18, 2019, a two-volume [redacted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitization_\(classified_information\)) version of the special counsel's report titled *[Report on the Investigation into Russian interference in the 2016 Presidential Election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Report_on_the_Investigation_into_Russian_interference_in_the_2016_Presidential_Election)* was released to Congress and to the public. About one-eighth of the lines in the public version were redacted.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287]

[Volume I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_Report#Volume_I) discusses about Russian interference in the 2016 presidential election, concluding that interference occurred "in sweeping and systematic fashion" and "violated U.S. criminal law".[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] The report detailed activities by the [Internet Research Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_Research_Agency), a Kremlin-linked Russian [troll farm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troll_farm), to create a "social media campaign that favored presidential candidate Donald J. Trump and disparaged presidential candidate Hillary Clinton",[^cite_note-AFPpoints-290] and to "provoke and amplify political and social discord in the United States".[^cite_note-291] The report also described how the Russian intelligence service, the [GRU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GRU_\(G.U.\)), performed [computer hacking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_hacking) and strategic releasing of damaging material from the Clinton campaign and [Democratic Party](5043544.md) organizations.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293] To establish whether a crime was committed by members of the Trump campaign with regard to Russian interference, investigators used the legal standard for criminal [conspiracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_\(criminal\)) rather than the popular concept of "collusion", because a crime of "collusion" is not found in criminal law or the [United States Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Code).[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295]

According to the report, the investigation "identified numerous links between the Russian government and the Trump campaign", and found that Russia had "perceived it would benefit from a Trump presidency" and the 2016 Trump presidential campaign "expected it would benefit electorally" from Russian hacking efforts. Ultimately, "the investigation did not establish that members of the Trump campaign conspired or coordinated with the Russian government in its election interference activities."[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] However, investigators had an incomplete picture of what had really occurred during the 2016 campaign, due to some associates of the Trump campaign providing false or incomplete testimony, exercising the [privilege against self-incrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plead_the_Fifth), and having deleted, unsaved, or encrypted communications. As such, the Mueller report "cannot rule out the possibility" that information then unavailable to investigators would have presented different findings.[^cite_note-298]

[Volume II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_Report#Volume_II) covered obstruction of justice. The report described [ten episodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mueller_Report#Episodes_of_alleged_obstruction) where Trump may have obstructed justice as president, plus one instance before he was elected.[^cite_note-FactCheck11-299][^cite_note-300] The report said that in addition to Trump's public attacks on the investigation and its subjects, he had also privately tried to "control the investigation" in multiple ways, but mostly failed to influence it because his subordinates or associates refused to carry out his instructions.[^cite_note-NYTview-301][^cite_note-302] For that reason, no charges against the Trump's aides and associates were recommended "beyond those already filed".[^cite_note-FactCheck11-299] The special counsel could not charge Trump himself once investigators decided to abide by an [Office of Legal Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Legal_Counsel) (OLC) opinion that a sitting president cannot stand trial,[^cite_note-APnocall-303][^cite_note-TimeSay-304] and they feared charges would affect Trump's governing and possibly preempt his impeachment.[^cite_note-TimeSay-304][^cite_note-305] In addition, investigators felt it would be unfair to accuse Trump of a crime without charges and without a trial in which he could clear his name,[^cite_note-APnocall-303][^cite_note-TimeSay-304][^cite_note-NYTview-301] hence investigators "determined not to apply an approach that could potentially result in a judgment that the President committed crimes".[^cite_note-TimeSay-304][^cite_note-306][^cite_note-APdilemma-307][^cite_note-308]

Since the special counsel's office had decided "not to make a traditional prosecutorial judgment" on whether to "initiate or decline a prosecution", they "did not draw ultimate conclusions about the President's conduct". The report "does not conclude that the president committed a crime",[^cite_note-AFPpoints-290][^cite_note-309] but specifically did not exonerate Trump on obstruction of justice, because investigators were not confident that Trump was innocent after examining his intent and actions.[^cite_note-310][^cite_note-BBCeight-311] The report concluded "that Congress has authority to prohibit a President's corrupt use of his authority in order to protect the integrity of the administration of justice" and "that Congress may apply the obstruction laws to the president's corrupt exercise of the powers of office accords with our constitutional system of checks and balances and the principle that no person is above the law".[^cite_note-APdilemma-307][^cite_note-BBCeight-311][^cite_note-NYTview-301]

On May 1, 2019, following publication of the special counsel's report, Barr testified before the [Senate Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_the_Judiciary), during which Barr said he "didn't exonerate" Trump on obstruction as that was not the role of the Justice Department.[^cite_note-312] He declined to testify before the [House Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_Committee_on_the_Judiciary) the following day because he objected to the committee's plan to use staff lawyers during questioning.[^cite_note-313] Barr also repeatedly[^cite_note-314] failed to give the unredacted special counsel's report to the Judiciary Committee by its deadline of May 6, 2019.[^cite_note-315] On May 8, 2019, the committee voted to hold Barr in [contempt of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contempt_of_Congress), which refers the matter to entire House for resolution.[^cite_note-316] Concurrently, Trump asserted [executive privilege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_privilege) via the Department of Justice in an effort to prevent the redacted portions of the special counsel's report and the underlying evidence from being disclosed.[^cite_note-317] Committee chairman [Jerry Nadler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Nadler) said the U.S. is in a [constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_crisis), "because the President is disobeying the law, is refusing all information to Congress".[^cite_note-318] Speaker [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi) said Trump was "self-impeaching" by stonewalling Congress.[^cite_note-319]

Following release of the Mueller report, Trump and his allies turned their attention toward "investigating the investigators".[^cite_note-320] On May 23, 2019, Trump ordered the [intelligence community](501244.md) to cooperate with Barr's investigation of the origins of the investigation, granting Barr full authority to declassify any intelligence information related to the matter. Some analysts expressed concerns that the order could create a conflict between the Justice Department and the intelligence community over closely guarded intelligence sources and methods, as well as open the possibility Barr could cherrypick intelligence for public release to help Trump.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324]

Upon announcing the formal closure of the investigation and his resignation from the Justice Department on May 29, Mueller said, "If we had had confidence that the president clearly did not commit a crime, we would have said so. We did not, however, decide as to whether the president did commit a crime."[^cite_note-325] During his testimony to Congress on July 24, 2019, Mueller said that a president could be charged with obstruction of justice (or other crimes) after the president left office.[^cite_note-326]

### Counter-investigations

Main article: [Russia investigation origins conspiracy theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_investigation_origins_conspiracy_theory)

Amid accusations by Trump and his supporters that he had been subjected to an illegitimate investigation, in May 2019, Barr appointed federal prosecutor [John Durham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Durham_\(lawyer\)) to review the origins of the Crossfire Hurricane investigation.[^cite_note-327] By September 2020, Durham's inquiry had expanded to include the FBI's investigation of the Clinton Foundation during the 2016 campaign.[^cite_note-328]

In November 2017, Sessions appointed U.S. Attorney [John Huber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_W._Huber) to investigate the FBI's surveillance of [Carter Page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carter_Page) and [connections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Foundation%E2%80%93State_Department_controversy#Uranium_One) between the [Clinton Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Foundation) and [Uranium One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium_One), starting in November 2017.[^cite_note-329] The investigation ended in January 2020 after no evidence was found to warrant the opening of a criminal investigation.[^cite_note-330] Special Counsel Robert Mueller's April 2019 report documented that Trump pressured Sessions and the Department of Justice to reopen the investigation into Clinton's emails.[^cite_note-331]

## Ethics

See also: [Second presidency of Donald Trump § Ethics](78298108.md#Ethics), [Legal affairs of the first Trump presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_affairs_of_the_first_Trump_presidency), [2019 Trump–Zelenskyy phone call](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Trump%E2%80%93Zelenskyy_phone_call), [2019 Trump–Ukraine scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Trump%E2%80%93Ukraine_scandal), and [Donald Trump sexual misconduct allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_sexual_misconduct_allegations)

The Trump administration was characterized by a departure from ethical norms.[^cite_note-FloutNorms-332][^cite_note-333] Unlike previous administrations of both parties, the Trump White House did not observe a strict boundary between official government activities and personal, political, or campaign activities.[^cite_note-FloutNorms-332][^cite_note-Gomez-334][^cite_note-335] Critics went so far as to describe Trump as bringing [kleptocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kleptocracy) to America.[^cite_note-342]

### Role of lobbyists

Trump promised during his campaign to "[drain the swamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drain_the_swamp)" – referring to entrenched corruption and lobbying in Washington, D.C. – and proposed a series of ethics reforms. Among the administration's first policies was a five-year ban on serving as a lobbyist after working in the executive branch.[^cite_note-bschrekinger11182016-343] However, he rolled back that policy as one of his final acts in office.[^cite_note-344] According to federal records and interviews, lobbying during the presidency, particularly through Pence's office, nearly doubled.[^cite_note-wapopence-345]

### Potential conflicts of interest

See also: [Foreign Emoluments Clause § Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Emoluments_Clause#Trump_administration)



Map shows the number of companies owned by Donald Trump

[^cite_note-cnn-conflicts-346]

&#x20;that are operating in each country:

&#x20; 1-3

&#x20; 4-8

&#x20; 9-15

&#x20; Over 15

Trump's presidency caused concerns about [conflict of interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_of_interest) with his business ventures. He moved his businesses into a [revocable trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revocable_trust),[^cite_note-347][^cite_note-BBC041817-348] placing his sons, [Eric Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Trump) and [Donald Trump Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_Jr.), at the head of his businesses. However, critics said this would not prevent his input into his businesses and benefitting himself, and he continued to receive quarterly updates on his businesses.[^cite_note-349]

Ethics experts found his plan to address conflicts of interest between his presidency and his business interests to be inadequate. He did not put his business interests in a [blind trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blind_trust) or similar, unlike every other president in the last 40 years.[^cite_note-YourishBuchanan-350] He still owned stakes in hundreds of businesses a year into his presidency.[^cite_note-351] Journalist [Anne Applebaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne_Applebaum) reported how Trump properties raised conflicts of interest.[^cite_note-352] The *Washington Post* reported in a June 2019 analysis that federal officials and GOP campaigns had spent at least \$1.6 million at businesses owned by Trump during the first few months of his presidency.[^cite_note-Fahrenthold-2019-353] The newspaper reported in 2020 that the federal government was also charged over \$1 million over the course of the term for officials staying at the properties when Trump [visited his properties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidential_trips_made_by_Donald_Trump) well over 200 times during his presidency.[^cite_note-354]

[Citizens for Responsibility and Ethics in Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens_for_Responsibility_and_Ethics_in_Washington) (CREW),[^cite_note-Riback-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-357] the [District of Columbia and State of Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D.C._and_Maryland_v._Trump) jointly,[^cite_note-358][^cite_note-359] and over [200 Democratic members of congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blumenthal_v._Trump) initiated three separate lawsuits against Trump for violations of the [Foreign Emoluments Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Emoluments_Clause) in 2017.[^cite_note-360] [CREW v. Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CREW_v._Trump) was dismissed the same year for lack of [standing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_\(law\)).[^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362] The U.S. Supreme Court dismissed the remaining two lawsuits in January 2021 as Trump was no longer president.[^cite_note-363]

### Transparency, data availability, and record keeping

The Trump administration stopped the longstanding practice of logging visitors to the White House.[^cite_note-Eilperin-2017-364][^cite_note-365] [CNN](62028.md) reported in July 2018 that the White House had ceased publishing public summaries of Trump's phone calls with world leaders, which was common practice in previous administrations.[^cite_note-366] *The Washington Post* reported in May 2017 that information, such as "workplace violations, energy efficiency, and animal welfare abuses" had been removed or tucked away.[^cite_note-Eilperin-2017-364]

He refused to follow the [Presidential Records Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_Records_Act), which requires presidents and their administrations to preserve all official documents for the National Archives. He tore up papers after reading them, and staffers had to collect and tape them back together for the archives.[^cite_note-367] He also took boxes of documents and other items with him when he left the White House, which were later returned.[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369] Some of these documents were classified, including at the "top secret" level.[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] Trump sometimes used his personal cellphone to converse with world leaders to avoid record of the conversation.[^cite_note-372] Federal prosecutors empaneled a grand jury in May 2022 to investigate possible mishandling of documents by Trump and other officials in his White House.[^cite_note-373]

### Hatch Act violations

The [Office of Special Counsel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Special_Counsel) found 13 senior Trump administration officials in violation of the [Hatch Act of 1939](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatch_Act_of_1939), which restricts unelected government employees' involvement in politics, having demonstrated "willful disregard" for the Hatch Act, especially during the 2020 campaign.[^cite_note-FloutNorms-332][^cite_note-Gomez-334][^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375]

### Security clearances

[Tricia Newbold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tricia_Newbold), a White House employee working on security clearances, privately told the [House Oversight Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Oversight_Committee) in March 2019 that at least 25 Trump administration officials had been granted security clearances over the objections of career staffers. She also asserted that some of these officials' applications were rejected for "disqualifying issues", which were overturned with inadequate explanation.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378]

The House Oversight Committee subpoenaed former head of White House security clearances [Carl Kline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Kline_\(White_House_official\)), but the administration instructed him not to comply, asserting that the subpoena "unconstitutionally encroaches on fundamental executive branch interests".[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380] He eventually gave closed-door testimony before the committee in May 2019, but House Democrats said he did not "provide specific details to their questions".[^cite_note-381]

### Impeachment inquiry

Main articles: [Impeachment inquiry into Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_into_Donald_Trump) and [First impeachment of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump)

See also: [2019 Trump–Ukraine scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Trump%E2%80%93Ukraine_scandal)

An intelligence official privately filed a whistleblower complaint with the [Inspector General of the Intelligence Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inspector_General_of_the_Intelligence_Community), [Michael Atkinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Atkinson_\(inspector_general\)), on August 12, 2019.[^cite_note-382] The complaint alleged that in a July 2019 call, Trump had asked Ukrainian president [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](59210523.md) to investigate potential 2020 rival presidential candidate Joe Biden and his son [Hunter Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter_Biden) to improve his own electoral chances in 2020, as well as investigate the hacking of Democratic National Committee servers, which had been blamed on Russia. Additionally, the whistleblower alleged that the White House attempted to cover up the call records, and that the cancelling Vice President Mike Pence's May 2019 Ukraine trip, and withholding of financial aid from Ukraine was intended to pressure Zelensky.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384]

Inspector General Atkinson found the complaint credible and urgent, and transmitted it to acting [Director of National Intelligence](456082.md) [Joseph Maguire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Maguire) on August 26. By law, Maguire was supposed to forward the complaint to the Senate and House Intelligence Committees within a week, but refused, so Atkinson informed the committees of the existence of the complaint, but not its content.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-wapotut-386]

Trump confirmed on September 22 that he had discussed Biden and corruption with Zelensky, defending the call.[^cite_note-387] Trump also confirmed temporarily withholding aid from Ukraine, offering contradicting reasons for the decision.[^cite_note-388]



Open hearing testimony of [Fiona Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiona_Hill_\(presidential_advisor\)) and [David Holmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Holmes_\(diplomat\)) on November 21, 2019

A formal [impeachment inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_into_Donald_Trump) was announced on September 24.[^cite_note-389] The White House released a non-verbatim transcript of the [phone call between Trump and Zelensky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Trump%E2%80%93Zelenskyy_phone_call) the next day; while the congressional intelligence committees were allowed to read the whistleblower complaint. The White House declassified the complaint, which was released to the public.[^cite_note-wapotut-386] The transcript corroborated the main allegations of the complaint.[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391][^cite_note-392]

Congressional Democrats were generally in favor of impeachment, while Republicans defended the president.[^cite_note-393] Ukraine envoy [Kurt Volker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_Volker) resigned and three House committees issued a subpoena to Secretary of State Mike Pompeo to schedule depositions for Volker and four other State Department employees, and to compel the release of documents.[^cite_note-394][^cite_note-395]

### Use of the Office of President

Trump often sought to use the office of the presidency for his own interest. The Justice Department is traditionally independent from the president, but was used to act in Trump's interest during his presidency.[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398][^cite_note-399]

He directed [Postmaster General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postmaster_General) [Louis DeJoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_DeJoy) attempted to weaken the [U.S. postal service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Postal_Service) by cutting funding and services, to prevent postal votes from being counted during the 2020 election campaign.[^cite_note-400]

He fired, demoted, or withdrew nominations of numerous government officials for actions that harmed his image and personal or political interests, including FBI Director [James Comey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dismissal_of_James_Comey),[^cite_note-401] Deputy FBI Director [Andrew McCabe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_McCabe), U.S. Attorney General [Jeff Sessions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Sessions),[^cite_note-402] and Director of National Intelligence [Joseph Maguire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Maguire).[^cite_note-403]

According to several reports, his and his family's trips in the first month of his presidency cost U.S. taxpayers nearly as much as President Obama's travel expenses for an entire year, which was frequently criticized by Trump during his predecessor's term.[^cite_note-404] *The Washington Post* reported that his lavish lifestyle is far more expensive to the taxpayers than that of previous presidents.[^cite_note-405]

The [Department of Defense Office of Inspector General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Defense_Office_of_Inspector_General) issued an [investigation report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Medical_Unit#2024_Patient_Eligibility_and_Pharmaceutical_Management_Report) in January 2024 on prescription drug records and care between 2017 and 2019, which described improper recording of prescriptions, disposal of controlled substances, and verification of identities, among other problems. The pharmacy dispensed expensive [brand name drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proprietary_drug) for free, and considerable amounts of money was spent on healthcare for many ineligible White House staff members, employees, and contractors.[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408]

### 2018 midterm elections

Main article: [2018 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_United_States_elections)

In the 2018 mid-term elections, Democrats had a [blue wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_elections_in_the_United_States), [winning control of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections), while Republicans [expanded their majority in the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_United_States_Senate_elections).[^cite_note-412] After the election, Nancy Pelosi replaced Paul Ryan as Speaker of the House, while Kevin McCarthy became the House Minority Leader.

### 2020 presidential election

Main articles: [Donald Trump 2020 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign) and [2020 United States presidential election](48410011.md)

Further information: [2020 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_elections), [2020 Republican Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries), and [2020 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Republican_National_Convention)

Trump officially announced his [reelection campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign) for the [Republican nomination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries) in the [2020 presidential election](48410011.md) on June 18, 2019.[^cite_note-413] Trump did not face any significant rivals for the 2020 Republican nomination, with some state Republican parties cancelling the presidential primaries in the states.[^cite_note-414] Trump's Democratic opponent in the general election was former vice president [Joe Biden](145422.md) of Delaware. The election on November 3 was not called for either candidate for several days. On November 7, the Associated Press along with mainstream media called the race for Joe Biden.[^cite_note-415]

It was the first presidency since that of [Herbert Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbert_Hoover) in 1932 in which a sitting president was defeated and his party lost its majorities in both chambers of Congress.[^cite_note-416]

#### Lost reelection and transition period&#x20;

Main articles: [Presidential transition of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Joe_Biden), [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md), and [Trump fake electors plot](71378172.md)



Democrat [Joe Biden](145422.md) defeated Trump in the 2020 presidential election

Trump refused to concede, and the administration did not begin cooperating with president-elect Biden's transition team until November 23.[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418] In late December 2020, Biden and his transition team criticized Trump administration political appointees for hampering the transition and failing to cooperate with the Biden transition team on national security areas, such as the Defense and State departments, as well as on the economic response to the COVID-19 pandemic, saying that many of the agencies that are critical to their security have incurred enormous damage and have been hollowed out – in personnel, capacity and in morale.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-420]

Throughout December and January, Trump continued to insist that he had won the election. In December 2020, reports emerged that U.S. military leaders were on high alert, and ranking officers had discussed what to do if Trump declared [martial law](50477.md).[^cite_note-421] CIA director [Gina Haspel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gina_Haspel) and Army general [Mark Milley](38091005.md), chairman of the [Joint Chiefs of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Chiefs_of_Staff), grew concerned that Trump might attempt a [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup) or military action against China or Iran.[^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423] Milley insisted that he be consulted about any military orders from Trump, including the use of nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-425]

Trump filed numerous lawsuits alleging election fraud, tried to [persuade state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump%E2%80%93Raffensperger_phone_call) and federal officials to [overturn the results](65905202.md), and urged his supporters to rally on his behalf.[^cite_note-426] Although most resulting lawsuits were either dismissed or ruled against by numerous courts,[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] Trump nonetheless [conspired](3406102.md) with his [campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2020_presidential_campaign) team to [submit documents in several states](71378172.md) (all of which had been won by Biden) which [falsely claimed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Making_false_statements) to be legitimate electoral certificates for President Trump and Vice President [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence).[^cite_note-Breuninger_6/22/2022-430][^cite_note-431] After the submission of these documents, the Trump campaign intended that the [presiding officer of the United States Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presiding_Officer_of_the_United_States_Senate), either [President of the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Senate_of_the_United_States) Pence or [President pro tempore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_pro_tempore_of_the_United_States_Senate) [Chuck Grassley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Grassley), would claim to have the unilateral power to reject [electors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College#Electors) during the [January 6, 2021 vote counting session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count); the presiding officer would reject all electors from the several states in which the Trump campaign had submitted false documents, leaving 232 votes for Trump and 222 votes for Biden, thereby overturning the election results in favour of Trump.[^cite_note-NYT-20230808-432][^cite_note-433][^cite_note-434][^cite_note-435] The plans for January 6 failed to come to fruition after Pence refused to follow the campaign's proposals.[^cite_note-Showdown-436][^cite_note-437]

#### Electoral vote count and U.S. Capitol attack

Main articles: [2021 United States Electoral College vote count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count) and [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md)

Trump's statement during the U.S. Capitol attack on January 6, 2021. The video was originally posted on Twitter and shared on other social media before being removed from all platforms for violating various policies

On January 6, 2021, rioters supporting Trump [stormed](66307490.md) the [U.S. Capitol](31979.md) in an effort to thwart a [joint session of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_session_of_Congress) during which the [Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) vote was to be certified, affirming the election of former vice president [Joe Biden](145422.md) as president and Senator [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) as vice president.

During an initial rally earlier that morning, Trump encouraged his supporters to march to the U.S. Capitol.[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439] Subsequently, pro-Trump attendees marched to the Capitol building, joined other protesters, and stormed the building.[^cite_note-440] Congress was in session at the time, conducting the [Electoral College vote count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_States_Electoral_College_vote_count) and debating the results of the vote. As the protesters arrived, Capitol security evacuated the Senate and House of Representatives chambers and locked down several other buildings on the Capitol campus.[^cite_note-441] Later that evening, after the Capitol was secured, Congress went back into session to discuss the Electoral College vote, finally affirming at 3\:41 a.m. that Biden had won the election.[^cite_note-442]

Five casualties occurred during the event: one Capitol Police officer, and four stormers or protesters at the Capitol, including one rioter shot by police inside the building.[^cite_note-443] At least 138 police officers were injured.[^cite_note-444] Three [improvised explosive devices](265112.md) were reported to have been found: one each on Capitol grounds, at the [Republican National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_National_Committee) and [Democratic National Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Committee) offices.[^cite_note-445]

#### Aftermath

Main article: [Aftermath of the January 6 United States Capitol attack](66369984.md)

Following the Capitol attack, several cabinet-level officials and White House staff resigned, citing the incident and Trump's behavior.[^cite_note-446]

On January 7, the day after the Electoral College results were certified by Congress, Trump tweeted a video in which he stated, "A new administration will be inaugurated on January 20th. My focus now turns to ensuring a smooth, orderly and seamless transition of power."[^cite_note-447] The [State Department](31975.md) subsequently told diplomats to affirm Biden's victory.[^cite_note-448]

On January 12, the House voted in favor of requesting that the vice president remove Trump from office per the [Twenty-fifth Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-fifth_Amendment); hours earlier, Pence had indicated that he opposed such a measure.[^cite_note-449] The next day, the House voted 232–197 to [impeach Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_of_Donald_Trump) on a charge of "incitement of insurrection". Ten Republican representatives joined all Democratic representatives in voting to impeach Trump. Trump is the first and only president to be impeached twice.[^cite_note-450] On February 13, the Senate voted 57–43 to [convict Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_impeachment_trial_of_Donald_Trump) on a charge of inciting insurrection, ten votes short of the required two-thirds majority, and he was acquitted. Seven Republican senators joined all Democratic and independent senators in voting to convict Trump.[^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452]

President Trump's first farewell address on January 19, 2021

Trump gave a [first farewell address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump's_first_farewell_address) the day prior to the [inauguration of Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Joe_Biden). In it he stressed his economic and foreign policy record, and said the country can never tolerate "[political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence)".[^cite_note-453] Trump did not attend Biden's inauguration, becoming the first departing president in 152 years to refuse to attend his elected successor's inauguration,[^cite_note-454][^cite_note-455] but he did honor another tradition by leaving Biden a letter on the [*Resolute* desk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resolute_desk) in the White House.[^cite_note-456][^cite_note-457]

## Historical evaluations and public opinion

### Historical evaluations

Main article: [Historical rankings of presidents of the United States § Since 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States#Since_2018)



Despite claims he won "in a landslide", Trump won the 2016 election with 56.9% of the electoral college—placing the win in approximately the 23rd percentiles of all presidential elections.

[^cite_note-NYTimes_20161218-458]



Trump claimed he won the 2016 electoral vote in a "landslide", but he ranks below most presidents in both the electoral vote (then 46th out of 58 elections;

[^cite_note-NYTimes_20161218-458]

&#x20;accompanying chart) and popular vote (near the bottom of all elections; shown in chart

[^cite_note-UCSB_PopularVote-459]

).



The [V-Dem Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Institute) said regarding Trump's presidency that "the speed with which American democracy is currently dismantled is unprecedented in modern history".

[^cite_note-VDEM_Report_2026-460]

&#x20;First-term declines in the Institute's indices of democracy were substantial, to be exceeded only during his second term.

[^cite_note-VDEM_Report_2026-460]

In the 2018 presidential rankings by the Siena College Research Institute, Trump ranked as the third-worst president in history.[^cite_note-461] [C-SPAN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-SPAN)'s 2021 President Historians Survey ranked Trump as the fourth-worst president overall and the worst in the leadership characteristics of Moral Authority and [Administrative Skills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiring_and_personnel_concerns_about_Donald_Trump). Trump's best rated leadership characteristic was Public Persuasion, where he ranked 32nd out of the 44 presidents.[^cite_note-462] Trump ranked last in both the 2018 and 2024 surveys of the [American Political Science Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Political_Science_Association) Presidents and Executive Politics section, with self-identified Republican historians ranking Trump in their bottom five presidents.[^cite_note-463]

### Opinion polling

Main articles: [Opinion polling on the first Trump presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_on_the_first_Trump_presidency) and [United States presidential approval rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_approval_rating)



Gallup approval polling, Jan. 2017 – Jan. 2021&#x20;

&#x20; Disapprove

&#x20; Unsure

&#x20; Approve



In a larger context, Trump's approval rating ([Gallup polling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_polling)) after the first year of his first term was the lowest to date of any president since 1977, exceeded only by the rating of his second term's first year.

[^cite_note-NYTimes_20251226-464]

At the time of the 2016 election, polls by [Gallup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_\(company\)) found Trump had a favorable rating around 35 percent and an unfavorable rating around 60 percent, while Clinton held a favorable rating of 40 percent and an unfavorable rating of 57 percent.[^cite_note-465] 2016 was the first election cycle in modern presidential polling in which both major-party candidates were viewed so unfavorably.[^cite_note-466][^cite_note-467][^cite_note-468][^cite_note-469] By January 20, 2017, [Inauguration Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_inauguration), Trump's approval rating average was 42 percent, the lowest rating average for an incoming president in the history of modern polling;[^cite_note-470] during his term it was an "incredibly stable (and also historically low)" 36 percent to 40 percent.[^cite_note-471][^cite_note-472] According to Gallup, Trump's approval rating peaked at 49 percent in several polls in early 2020; this makes him the only president to never reach a 50 percent approval rating in the Gallup poll dating to 1938.[^cite_note-473]

### Democratic backsliding

Main article: [Democratic backsliding in the United States § Indicators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_the_United_States#Indicators)

Since the beginning of Trump's presidency, ratings of how well U.S. democracy is functioning has dropped significantly according to the 2018 [Varieties of Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-DEM) Annual Democracy Report, which cites "a significant [democratic backsliding in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_the_United_States) \[since the [Inauguration of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Donald_Trump)] ... attributable to weakening constraints on the executive."[^cite_note-Varieties_of_Democracy_Project_VDEM-2018-474] [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) also attributed a 2019 decrease in its U.S. rankings to Trump, as did [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) in downgrading the United States in its [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index).[^cite_note-475] [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_IDEA) labeled the U.S. a "backsliding democracy" after evaluating 2020 and 2021 events, noting [Trump's election denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_denial_movement_in_the_United_States) as a historic turning point and the [Jan. 6 attack on the U.S. Capitol](66307490.md) as raising alarm bells.[^cite_note-476]

[^cite_note-293]: &#x20;*[Mueller Report](https://www.justice.gov/storage/report.pdf) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20190419072437/https://www.justice.gov/storage/report.pdf) April 19, 2019, at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine)*, vol. I, p. 4: At the same time the IRA operation began to focus on supporting candidate Trump in early 2016, the Russian government employed a second form of interference: cyber intrusions (hacking) and releases of hacked materials damaging to the Clinton Campaign. The Russian intelligence service known as the Main Intelligence Directorate of the General Staff of the Russian Army (GRU) carried out these operations. In March 2016, the GRU began hacking the email accounts of Clinton Campaign volunteers and employees, including campaign chairman John Podesta. In April 2016, the GRU hacked into the computer networks of the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee (DCCC) and the Democratic National Committee (DNC). The GRU stole hundreds of thousands of documents from the compromised email accounts and networks. Around the time the DNC announced in mid-June 2016 the Russian government's role in hacking its network, the GRU began disseminating stolen materials through the fictitious online personas "DCLeaks" and "Guccifer 2.0". The GRU later released additional materials through the organization WikiLeaks.

[^cite_note-294]: &#x20;Morais, Betsy (April 18, 2019). ["Collusion by any other name"](https://www.cjr.org/first_person/trump-mueller-collusion.php). *[Columbia Journalism Review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Journalism_Review)*. Retrieved April 23, 2019.

[^cite_note-295]: &#x20;*[Mueller Report](https://www.justice.gov/storage/report.pdf) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20190419072437/https://www.justice.gov/storage/report.pdf) April 19, 2019, at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine)*, vol. I, p. 2: In evaluating whether evidence about collective action of multiple individuals constituted a crime, we applied the framework of conspiracy law, not the concept of "collusion". In so doing, the Office recognized that the word "collud\[e]" was used in communications with the Acting Attorney General confirming certain aspects of the investigation's scope and that the term has frequently been invoked in public reporting about the investigation. But collusion is not a specific offense or theory of liability found in the United States Code, nor is it a term of art in federal criminal law. For those reasons, the Office's focus in analyzing questions of joint criminal liability was on conspiracy as defined in federal law.
